Like during client interviews—I’m a private investigator, so listening is kind of the whole job—and I’ll suddenly be standing or moving around, and then I realize the person noticed.\n\nIt’s not just work, either. I do tutoring sometimes, and even at book club, which should be easy, right? We’re just sitting there talking about a novel, and I’m up getting water or checking something or pacing a little. Anyway, people have started commenting that I can’t just sit and listen, and it’s getting embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, when did you first start noticing this restlessness and difficulty staying seated—was it something you struggled with even as a child, or did it start more recently, say in the past several months or years?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn’t just start recently. I mean, I can make it sound like a “work stress” thing now, because my job is kind of… you know, waiting around and watching people and then suddenly needing to act, which is a weird combination. But honestly, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards used to say stuff like I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother still laughs about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. I mean, I can make it sound like a “work stress” thing now, because my job is kind of… you know, waiting around and watching people and then suddenly needing to act, which is a weird combination. But honestly, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards used to say stuff like I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother still laughs about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. Which is funny now, sort of, but also… yeah, it was a thing even then.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus on tasks, forgetting details, or your mind wandering when you're supposed to be concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. The thing I’m most aware of is the restlessness, because other people can see it and they react to it. Like, standing up in the middle of something is hard to miss.\n\nWith focus or forgetting details… I don’t know how to separate that from normal life, if that makes sense. I mean, everybody’s mind wanders sometimes. Mine might, but I haven’t really been tracking it the way I notice the getting up and moving around. That’s the part that’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks or conversations to the point that you lose track of what was being said or done, even when you're not moving around?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know if I’d say “often,” because I’m not sure I notice it that way. Like, if I’m sitting still—which, honestly, is already kind of the challenge—then I’m usually trying so hard to look like I’m listening that I don’t always know what’s happening in my head.

Sometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking about, I don’t know, the lighting in the room or whether I parked somewhere I shouldn’t have, but then again that feels like something everyone does. And in my line of work you pick up random details, so it’s hard to tell what’s distraction and what’s just… noticing things.

So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. The losing-track part isn’t what I came in thinking about. It’s more that people see me moving around and then I feel self-conscious.

Like, standing up in the middle of something is hard to miss.\n\nWith focus or forgetting details… I don’t know how to separate that from normal life, if that makes sense. I mean, everybody’s mind wanders sometimes. Mine might, but I haven’t really been tracking it the way I notice the getting up and moving around. That’s the part that’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks or conversations to the point that you lose track of what was being said or done, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “often,” because I’m not sure I notice it that way. Like, if I’m sitting still—which, honestly, is already kind of the challenge—then I’m usually trying so hard to look like I’m listening that I don’t always know what’s happening in my head.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking about, I don’t know, the lighting in the room or whether I parked somewhere I shouldn’t have, but then again that feels like something everyone does. And in my line of work you pick up random details, so it’s hard to tell what’s distraction and what’s just… noticing things.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. The losing-track part isn’t what I came in thinking about. It’s more that people see me moving around and then I feel self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, have you had ongoing problems with things like getting into conflicts, breaking rules, being defiant with authority figures, or acting aggressively toward others—either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. In my work you run into tense people, and sometimes people don’t exactly appreciate being watched or asked questions, but that’s not really the same as… whatever you mean clinically.\n\nAs a kid, I don’t remember being, like, out to fight authority. I probably annoyed teachers because I couldn’t just sit there like a statue, but breaking rules or being aggressive—nothing stands out to me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that one, yes. I do that more than I probably want to admit.

Like if I hear the younger detectives talking through a lead, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” before anyone asked me. Or even with neighbors in the hallway—someone’s talking about something and I’m suddenly in the conversation with questions or advice, and then a second later I’m like, oh, I just barged into that.

It’s not usually meant to be rude. It’s more like the thought is already out of my mouth before I’ve checked whether it’s my turn to say it.

Anyway, people have started commenting that I can’t just sit and listen, and it’s getting embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, when did you first start noticing this restlessness and difficulty staying seated—was it something you struggled with even as a child, or did it start more recently, say in the past several months or years?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn’t just start recently. I mean, I can make it sound like a “work stress” thing now, because my job is kind of… you know, waiting around and watching people and then suddenly needing to act, which is a weird combination. But honestly, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards used to say stuff like I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother still laughs about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. Which is funny now, sort of, but also… yeah, it was a thing even then.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus on tasks, forgetting details, or your mind wandering when you're supposed to be concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. The thing I’m most aware of is the restlessness, because other people can see it and they react to it. Like, standing up in the middle of something is hard to miss.\n\nWith focus or forgetting details… I don’t know how to separate that from normal life, if that makes sense. I mean, everybody’s mind wanders sometimes. Mine might, but I haven’t really been tracking it the way I notice the getting up and moving around. That’s the part that’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks or conversations to the point that you lose track of what was being said or done, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “often,” because I’m not sure I notice it that way. Like, if I’m sitting still—which, honestly, is already kind of the challenge—then I’m usually trying so hard to look like I’m listening that I don’t always know what’s happening in my head.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking about, I don’t know, the lighting in the room or whether I parked somewhere I shouldn’t have, but then again that feels like something everyone does. And in my line of work you pick up random details, so it’s hard to tell what’s distraction and what’s just… noticing things.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. The losing-track part isn’t what I came in thinking about. It’s more that people see me moving around and then I feel self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, have you had ongoing problems with things like getting into conflicts, breaking rules, being defiant with authority figures, or acting aggressively toward others—either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “conflicts” is a big word, right? In my work you run into tense people, and sometimes people don’t exactly appreciate being watched or asked questions, but that’s not really the same as… whatever you mean clinically.\n\nAs a kid, I don’t remember being, like, out to fight authority. I probably annoyed teachers because I couldn’t just sit there like a statue, but breaking rules or being aggressive—nothing stands out to me. My mother would probably have a whole more dramatic version, because she remembers every parent-teacher conference like it was a Supreme Court hearing, but… no, I don’t think that was the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. I do that more than I probably want to admit.\n\nLike if I hear the younger detectives talking through a lead, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” before anyone asked me. Or even with neighbors in the hallway—someone’s talking about something and I’m suddenly in the conversation with questions or advice, and then a second later I’m like, oh, I just barged into that.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude. It’s more like the thought is already out of my mouth before I’ve checked whether it’s my turn to say it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. In my work, details are kind of the whole job, so if I miss something, it’s not usually obvious right away—or I tell myself it’s because the situation was messy, or the client gave me half the information, which happens more than you’d think.

Organizing tasks… I mean, I have systems. Not pretty systems, but systems. Notes in different places, reminders, little scraps—actually I found a receipt yesterday with half a license plate written on it, and I spent ten minutes trying to remember if it was important or just from some guy who cut me off in traffic.

So… I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes exactly. I keep track of what I need to do somehow, but it may not look very organized from the outside.

Not pretty systems, but systems. I don’t think so. I mean, lying is a funny one in my line of work—people lie to me constantly, and sometimes I’m not exactly announcing every thought in my head when I’m trying to get information, but that’s not the same as, you know, some lifelong pattern of lying to hurt people or get away with things.

It’s more like the thought is already out of my mouth before I’ve checked whether it’s my turn to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or overlook details in your work, or have trouble organizing tasks and keeping track of things you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. In my work, details are kind of the whole job, so if I miss something, it’s not usually obvious right away—or I tell myself it’s because the situation was messy, or the client gave me half the information, which happens more than you’d think.\n\nOrganizing tasks… I mean, I have systems. Not pretty systems, but systems. Notes in different places, reminders, little scraps—actually I found a receipt yesterday with half a license plate written on it, and I spent ten minutes trying to remember if it was important or just from some guy who cut me off in traffic.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes exactly. I keep track of what I need to do somehow, but it may not look very organized from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a pattern of behaviors like lying, stealing, destroying property, or being physically cruel to people or animals, either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, lying is a funny one in my line of work—people lie to me constantly, and sometimes I’m not exactly announcing every thought in my head when I’m trying to get information, but that’s not the same as, you know, some lifelong pattern of lying to hurt people or get away with things.\n\nStealing, destroying property, cruelty—no, nothing like that comes to mind. I had a dog growing up, actually, a very stubborn little terrier, and if anything I was the one sneaking him extra food when I wasn’t supposed to. My mother used to say he listened better than I did, which was probably true.\n\nBut no, I don’t remember anything like being physically cruel or breaking things on purpose or stealing. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know… outside of work, it’s harder to say because there isn’t the same structure. With work, there’s a file, a client, a reason I have to keep things in one place—or at least pretend they’re in one place.

I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I guess what finally made me come in is that I keep getting up when I’m supposed to just stay seated. I do tutoring sometimes, and even at book club, which should be easy, right? We’re just sitting there talking about a novel, and I’m up getting water or checking something or pacing a little. Anyway, people have started commenting that I can’t just sit and listen, and it’s getting embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, when did you first start noticing this restlessness and difficulty staying seated—was it something you struggled with even as a child, or did it start more recently, say in the past several months or years?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn’t just start recently. I mean, I can make it sound like a “work stress” thing now, because my job is kind of… you know, waiting around and watching people and then suddenly needing to act, which is a weird combination. But honestly, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards used to say stuff like I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother still laughs about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. I do that more than I probably want to admit.\n\nLike if I hear the younger detectives talking through a lead, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” before anyone asked me. Or even with neighbors in the hallway—someone’s talking about something and I’m suddenly in the conversation with questions or advice, and then a second later I’m like, oh, I just barged into that.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude. It’s more like the thought is already out of my mouth before I’ve checked whether it’s my turn to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or overlook details in your work, or have trouble organizing tasks and keeping track of things you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. In my work, details are kind of the whole job, so if I miss something, it’s not usually obvious right away—or I tell myself it’s because the situation was messy, or the client gave me half the information, which happens more than you’d think.\n\nOrganizing tasks… I mean, I have systems. Not pretty systems, but systems. Notes in different places, reminders, little scraps—actually I found a receipt yesterday with half a license plate written on it, and I spent ten minutes trying to remember if it was important or just from some guy who cut me off in traffic.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes exactly. I keep track of what I need to do somehow, but it may not look very organized from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a pattern of behaviors like lying, stealing, destroying property, or being physically cruel to people or animals, either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, lying is a funny one in my line of work—people lie to me constantly, and sometimes I’m not exactly announcing every thought in my head when I’m trying to get information, but that’s not the same as, you know, some lifelong pattern of lying to hurt people or get away with things.\n\nStealing, destroying property, cruelty—no, nothing like that comes to mind. I had a dog growing up, actually, a very stubborn little terrier, and if anything I was the one sneaking him extra food when I wasn’t supposed to. My mother used to say he listened better than I did, which was probably true.\n\nBut no, I don’t remember anything like being physically cruel or breaking things on purpose or stealing. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget appointments, misplace important items like keys or your phone, or forget to complete tasks you had planned to do, in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… outside of work, it’s harder to say because there isn’t the same structure. With work, there’s a file, a client, a reason I have to keep things in one place—or at least pretend they’re in one place.\n\nAt home, if I put something down, sometimes I’ll find it in a completely different spot than I expected, but then again I live alone, so there’s no one else to blame. Well, except my cat, and she does knock things off tables, but she’s not exactly hiding my phone with intent.\n\nAppointments… I try to write them down. Whether I always remember where I wrote them is another matter. Sorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. I guess I’m not sure if it’s “often” or just normal life messiness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure. I mean, my energy goes up and down depending on what’s happening—if I’m chasing something on a case or waiting on somebody to call me back, I can get wired, but that feels more like the situation than some separate… episode.

But honestly, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards used to say stuff like I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother still laughs about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. Which is funny now, sort of, but also… yeah, it was a thing even then.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus on tasks, forgetting details, or your mind wandering when you're supposed to be concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. The thing I’m most aware of is the restlessness, because other people can see it and they react to it. Like, standing up in the middle of something is hard to miss.\n\nWith focus or forgetting details… I don’t know how to separate that from normal life, if that makes sense. I mean, everybody’s mind wanders sometimes. Mine might, but I haven’t really been tracking it the way I notice the getting up and moving around. That’s the part that’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks or conversations to the point that you lose track of what was being said or done, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “often,” because I’m not sure I notice it that way. Like, if I’m sitting still—which, honestly, is already kind of the challenge—then I’m usually trying so hard to look like I’m listening that I don’t always know what’s happening in my head.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking about, I don’t know, the lighting in the room or whether I parked somewhere I shouldn’t have, but then again that feels like something everyone does. And in my line of work you pick up random details, so it’s hard to tell what’s distraction and what’s just… noticing things.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. The losing-track part isn’t what I came in thinking about. It’s more that people see me moving around and then I feel self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, have you had ongoing problems with things like getting into conflicts, breaking rules, being defiant with authority figures, or acting aggressively toward others—either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “conflicts” is a big word, right? In my work you run into tense people, and sometimes people don’t exactly appreciate being watched or asked questions, but that’s not really the same as… whatever you mean clinically.\n\nAs a kid, I don’t remember being, like, out to fight authority. I probably annoyed teachers because I couldn’t just sit there like a statue, but breaking rules or being aggressive—nothing stands out to me. My mother would probably have a whole more dramatic version, because she remembers every parent-teacher conference like it was a Supreme Court hearing, but… no, I don’t think that was the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. I do that more than I probably want to admit.\n\nLike if I hear the younger detectives talking through a lead, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” before anyone asked me. Or even with neighbors in the hallway—someone’s talking about something and I’m suddenly in the conversation with questions or advice, and then a second later I’m like, oh, I just barged into that.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude. It’s more like the thought is already out of my mouth before I’ve checked whether it’s my turn to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or overlook details in your work, or have trouble organizing tasks and keeping track of things you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. In my work, details are kind of the whole job, so if I miss something, it’s not usually obvious right away—or I tell myself it’s because the situation was messy, or the client gave me half the information, which happens more than you’d think.\n\nOrganizing tasks… I mean, I have systems. Not pretty systems, but systems. Notes in different places, reminders, little scraps—actually I found a receipt yesterday with half a license plate written on it, and I spent ten minutes trying to remember if it was important or just from some guy who cut me off in traffic.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes exactly. I keep track of what I need to do somehow, but it may not look very organized from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a pattern of behaviors like lying, stealing, destroying property, or being physically cruel to people or animals, either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, lying is a funny one in my line of work—people lie to me constantly, and sometimes I’m not exactly announcing every thought in my head when I’m trying to get information, but that’s not the same as, you know, some lifelong pattern of lying to hurt people or get away with things.\n\nStealing, destroying property, cruelty—no, nothing like that comes to mind. I had a dog growing up, actually, a very stubborn little terrier, and if anything I was the one sneaking him extra food when I wasn’t supposed to. My mother used to say he listened better than I did, which was probably true.\n\nBut no, I don’t remember anything like being physically cruel or breaking things on purpose or stealing. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget appointments, misplace important items like keys or your phone, or forget to complete tasks you had planned to do, in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… outside of work, it’s harder to say because there isn’t the same structure. With work, there’s a file, a client, a reason I have to keep things in one place—or at least pretend they’re in one place.\n\nAt home, if I put something down, sometimes I’ll find it in a completely different spot than I expected, but then again I live alone, so there’s no one else to blame. Well, except my cat, and she does knock things off tables, but she’s not exactly hiding my phone with intent.\n\nAppointments… I try to write them down. Whether I always remember where I wrote them is another matter. Sorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. I guess I’m not sure if it’s “often” or just normal life messiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had distinct periods—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or had racing thoughts, separate from how you feel most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, my energy goes up and down depending on what’s happening—if I’m chasing something on a case or waiting on somebody to call me back, I can get wired, but that feels more like the situation than some separate… episode.\n\nLess sleep, sure, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say I suddenly don’t need it. It’s more like I stay up too late and then regret it, which is different, I think.\n\nRacing thoughts… my thoughts are usually pretty busy anyway. Sorry, I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t know if I’ve had a distinct several-day stretch that was really different from me. Nothing stands out like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I mean, paperwork is nobody’s favorite part of the job, right? Detailed reports can sit there looking at me like they’re judging me, but sometimes that’s just because I know once I start I’m going to find three things I should’ve written down earlier.

Reading long paperwork… it depends what it is. If it’s interesting, or if there’s something in it I’m trying to catch, I can get through it. If it’s dry, legal-type language, my eyes start doing that thing where they move over the words but I’m not sure anything is actually going in. Though that may just be because whoever writes those forms hates humanity.

So… I don’t know. I probably put it off sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s “often” in the way you mean.

I’m not sure. People sometimes say my mind is somewhere else, but that could mean a lot of things, couldn’t it? In my line of work, half the time I’m listening to someone and also watching the door, or noticing what they didn’t say, or wondering why their story changed from Tuesday to Thursday.

Anyway, people have started commenting that I can’t just sit and listen, and it’s getting embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, when did you first start noticing this restlessness and difficulty staying seated—was it something you struggled with even as a child, or did it start more recently, say in the past several months or years?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn’t just start recently. I mean, I can make it sound like a “work stress” thing now, because my job is kind of… you know, waiting around and watching people and then suddenly needing to act, which is a weird combination. But honestly, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards used to say stuff like I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother still laughs about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. Which is funny now, sort of, but also… yeah, it was a thing even then.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus on tasks, forgetting details, or your mind wandering when you're supposed to be concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. The thing I’m most aware of is the restlessness, because other people can see it and they react to it. Like, standing up in the middle of something is hard to miss.\n\nWith focus or forgetting details… I don’t know how to separate that from normal life, if that makes sense. I mean, everybody’s mind wanders sometimes. Mine might, but I haven’t really been tracking it the way I notice the getting up and moving around. That’s the part that’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks or conversations to the point that you lose track of what was being said or done, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “often,” because I’m not sure I notice it that way. Like, if I’m sitting still—which, honestly, is already kind of the challenge—then I’m usually trying so hard to look like I’m listening that I don’t always know what’s happening in my head.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking about, I don’t know, the lighting in the room or whether I parked somewhere I shouldn’t have, but then again that feels like something everyone does. And in my line of work you pick up random details, so it’s hard to tell what’s distraction and what’s just… noticing things.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. The losing-track part isn’t what I came in thinking about. It’s more that people see me moving around and then I feel self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, have you had ongoing problems with things like getting into conflicts, breaking rules, being defiant with authority figures, or acting aggressively toward others—either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “conflicts” is a big word, right? In my work you run into tense people, and sometimes people don’t exactly appreciate being watched or asked questions, but that’s not really the same as… whatever you mean clinically.\n\nAs a kid, I don’t remember being, like, out to fight authority. I probably annoyed teachers because I couldn’t just sit there like a statue, but breaking rules or being aggressive—nothing stands out to me. My mother would probably have a whole more dramatic version, because she remembers every parent-teacher conference like it was a Supreme Court hearing, but… no, I don’t think that was the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. I do that more than I probably want to admit.\n\nLike if I hear the younger detectives talking through a lead, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” before anyone asked me. Or even with neighbors in the hallway—someone’s talking about something and I’m suddenly in the conversation with questions or advice, and then a second later I’m like, oh, I just barged into that.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude. It’s more like the thought is already out of my mouth before I’ve checked whether it’s my turn to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or overlook details in your work, or have trouble organizing tasks and keeping track of things you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. In my work, details are kind of the whole job, so if I miss something, it’s not usually obvious right away—or I tell myself it’s because the situation was messy, or the client gave me half the information, which happens more than you’d think.\n\nOrganizing tasks… I mean, I have systems. Not pretty systems, but systems. Notes in different places, reminders, little scraps—actually I found a receipt yesterday with half a license plate written on it, and I spent ten minutes trying to remember if it was important or just from some guy who cut me off in traffic.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes exactly. I keep track of what I need to do somehow, but it may not look very organized from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a pattern of behaviors like lying, stealing, destroying property, or being physically cruel to people or animals, either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, lying is a funny one in my line of work—people lie to me constantly, and sometimes I’m not exactly announcing every thought in my head when I’m trying to get information, but that’s not the same as, you know, some lifelong pattern of lying to hurt people or get away with things.\n\nStealing, destroying property, cruelty—no, nothing like that comes to mind. I had a dog growing up, actually, a very stubborn little terrier, and if anything I was the one sneaking him extra food when I wasn’t supposed to. My mother used to say he listened better than I did, which was probably true.\n\nBut no, I don’t remember anything like being physically cruel or breaking things on purpose or stealing. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget appointments, misplace important items like keys or your phone, or forget to complete tasks you had planned to do, in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… outside of work, it’s harder to say because there isn’t the same structure. With work, there’s a file, a client, a reason I have to keep things in one place—or at least pretend they’re in one place.\n\nAt home, if I put something down, sometimes I’ll find it in a completely different spot than I expected, but then again I live alone, so there’s no one else to blame. Well, except my cat, and she does knock things off tables, but she’s not exactly hiding my phone with intent.\n\nAppointments… I try to write them down. Whether I always remember where I wrote them is another matter. Sorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. I guess I’m not sure if it’s “often” or just normal life messiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had distinct periods—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or had racing thoughts, separate from how you feel most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, my energy goes up and down depending on what’s happening—if I’m chasing something on a case or waiting on somebody to call me back, I can get wired, but that feels more like the situation than some separate… episode.\n\nLess sleep, sure, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say I suddenly don’t need it. It’s more like I stay up too late and then regret it, which is different, I think.\n\nRacing thoughts… my thoughts are usually pretty busy anyway. Sorry, I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t know if I’ve had a distinct several-day stretch that was really different from me. Nothing stands out like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid, put off, or feel reluctant to start tasks that require a lot of sustained mental effort—like writing up detailed reports or reading through long paperwork—because you find it hard to stay focused on them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I mean, paperwork is nobody’s favorite part of the job, right? Detailed reports can sit there looking at me like they’re judging me, but sometimes that’s just because I know once I start I’m going to find three things I should’ve written down earlier.\n\nReading long paperwork… it depends what it is. If it’s interesting, or if there’s something in it I’m trying to catch, I can get through it. If it’s dry, legal-type language, my eyes start doing that thing where they move over the words but I’m not sure anything is actually going in. Though that may just be because whoever writes those forms hates humanity.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I probably put it off sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do people often tell you that you seem to not be listening or that you drift off mentally when they’re speaking directly to you, even in moments when you’re not physically restless or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. People sometimes say my mind is somewhere else, but that could mean a lot of things, couldn’t it? In my line of work, half the time I’m listening to someone and also watching the door, or noticing what they didn’t say, or wondering why their story changed from Tuesday to Thursday.\n\nBut if you mean like… someone is talking right at me and I’m just gone? I don’t know that people tell me that often. My sister has said I get this look like I’m “three blocks away,” but she also says that when she’s telling me about neighborhood politics, and honestly there’s only so much one person can retain about parking disputes.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0"], "note": "No evidence of conduct disorder features (lying, stealing, aggression, defiance) ruled out F90.1. Clear childhood-onset hyperactivity/impulsivity (restlessness, blurting out, interrupting) with some inattentive traits supports F90.0 (Disturbance of activity and attention) rather than hyperkinetic conduct disorder.", "is_final": false}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Hyperactive/Impulsive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had distinct periods—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually energetic, needed much less sleep than normal, or had racing thoughts, separate from how you feel most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, my energy goes up and down depending on what’s happening—if I’m chasing something on a case or waiting on somebody to call me back, I can get wired, but that feels more like the situation than some separate… episode.\n\nLess sleep, sure, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say I suddenly don’t need it. It’s more like I stay up too late and then regret it, which is different, I think.\n\nRacing thoughts… my thoughts are usually pretty busy anyway. Sorry, I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t know if I’ve had a distinct several-day stretch that was really different from me. Nothing stands out like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often avoid, put off, or feel reluctant to start tasks that require a lot of sustained mental effort—like writing up detailed reports or reading through long paperwork—because you find it hard to stay focused on them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I mean, paperwork is nobody’s favorite part of the job, right? Detailed reports can sit there looking at me like they’re judging me, but sometimes that’s just because I know once I start I’m going to find three things I should’ve written down earlier.\n\nReading long paperwork… it depends what it is. If it’s interesting, or if there’s something in it I’m trying to catch, I can get through it. If it’s dry, legal-type language, my eyes start doing that thing where they move over the words but I’m not sure anything is actually going in. Though that may just be because whoever writes those forms hates humanity.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I probably put it off sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do people often tell you that you seem to not be listening or that you drift off mentally when they’re speaking directly to you, even in moments when you’re not physically restless or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. People sometimes say my mind is somewhere else, but that could mean a lot of things, couldn’t it? In my line of work, half the time I’m listening to someone and also watching the door, or noticing what they didn’t say, or wondering why their story changed from Tuesday to Thursday.\n\nBut if you mean like… someone is talking right at me and I’m just gone? I don’t know that people tell me that often. My sister has said I get this look like I’m “three blocks away,” but she also says that when she’s telling me about neighborhood politics, and honestly there’s only so much one person can retain about parking disputes.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say these patterns—being unable to sit still, interrupting others, or jumping in before your turn—cause noticeable problems for you in at least two different areas of your life, like both at work and in your personal relationships or home life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It's starting to cost me professionally—I've missed details in investigations, irritated clients, and made junior detectives less willing to debrief with me because they feel steamrolled.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.

At work it’s starting to cost me. I’ve missed details in investigations because I jumped ahead or pushed too fast, and I’ve irritated clients—sometimes I can tell I’ve cut them off before they got to the part they thought mattered. And the junior detectives… some of them don’t want to debrief with me as much, I think, because they feel steamrolled. One of them actually used that word, which stuck with me.

Personal life… I mean, people close to me have their own opinions about how I come across, sure, but professionally is where it’s been hardest to brush off. I used to think it was just being sharp or efficient, but lately it’s more like I’m leaving dents.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… I guess what finally made me come in is that I keep getting up when I’m supposed to just stay seated. Like during client interviews—I’m a private investigator, so listening is kind of the whole job—and I’ll suddenly be standing or moving around, and then I realize the person noticed.\n\nIt’s not just work, either. I do tutoring sometimes, and even at book club, which should be easy, right? We’re just sitting there talking about a novel, and I’m up getting water or checking something or pacing a little. Anyway, people have started commenting that I can’t just sit and listen, and it’s getting embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, when did you first start noticing this restlessness and difficulty staying seated—was it something you struggled with even as a child, or did it start more recently, say in the past several months or years?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn’t just start recently. I mean, I can make it sound like a “work stress” thing now, because my job is kind of… you know, waiting around and watching people and then suddenly needing to act, which is a weird combination. But honestly, this goes way back.\n\nMy grade-school report cards used to say stuff like I was bright, but I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother still laughs about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through. Which is funny now, sort of, but also… yeah, it was a thing even then.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the physical restlessness, do you also notice problems like losing focus on tasks, forgetting details, or your mind wandering when you're supposed to be concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. The thing I’m most aware of is the restlessness, because other people can see it and they react to it. Like, standing up in the middle of something is hard to miss.\n\nWith focus or forgetting details… I don’t know how to separate that from normal life, if that makes sense. I mean, everybody’s mind wanders sometimes. Mine might, but I haven’t really been tracking it the way I notice the getting up and moving around. That’s the part that’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks or conversations to the point that you lose track of what was being said or done, even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say “often,” because I’m not sure I notice it that way. Like, if I’m sitting still—which, honestly, is already kind of the challenge—then I’m usually trying so hard to look like I’m listening that I don’t always know what’s happening in my head.\n\nSometimes I’ll realize I’m thinking about, I don’t know, the lighting in the room or whether I parked somewhere I shouldn’t have, but then again that feels like something everyone does. And in my line of work you pick up random details, so it’s hard to tell what’s distraction and what’s just… noticing things.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. The losing-track part isn’t what I came in thinking about. It’s more that people see me moving around and then I feel self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, have you had ongoing problems with things like getting into conflicts, breaking rules, being defiant with authority figures, or acting aggressively toward others—either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “conflicts” is a big word, right? In my work you run into tense people, and sometimes people don’t exactly appreciate being watched or asked questions, but that’s not really the same as… whatever you mean clinically.\n\nAs a kid, I don’t remember being, like, out to fight authority. I probably annoyed teachers because I couldn’t just sit there like a statue, but breaking rules or being aggressive—nothing stands out to me. My mother would probably have a whole more dramatic version, because she remembers every parent-teacher conference like it was a Supreme Court hearing, but… no, I don’t think that was the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out answers or comments before someone finishes speaking, or having trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. I do that more than I probably want to admit.\n\nLike if I hear the younger detectives talking through a lead, I’ll just jump in with, “Did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” before anyone asked me. Or even with neighbors in the hallway—someone’s talking about something and I’m suddenly in the conversation with questions or advice, and then a second later I’m like, oh, I just barged into that.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude. It’s more like the thought is already out of my mouth before I’ve checked whether it’s my turn to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or overlook details in your work, or have trouble organizing tasks and keeping track of things you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. In my work, details are kind of the whole job, so if I miss something, it’s not usually obvious right away—or I tell myself it’s because the situation was messy, or the client gave me half the information, which happens more than you’d think.\n\nOrganizing tasks… I mean, I have systems. Not pretty systems, but systems. Notes in different places, reminders, little scraps—actually I found a receipt yesterday with half a license plate written on it, and I spent ten minutes trying to remember if it was important or just from some guy who cut me off in traffic.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes exactly. I keep track of what I need to do somehow, but it may not look very organized from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a pattern of behaviors like lying, stealing, destroying property, or being physically cruel to people or animals, either now or when you were younger?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, lying is a funny one in my line of work—people lie to me constantly, and sometimes I’m not exactly announcing every thought in my head when I’m trying to get information, but that’s not the same as, you know, some lifelong pattern of lying to hurt people or get away with things.\n\nStealing, destroying property, cruelty—no, nothing like that comes to mind. I had a dog growing up, actually, a very stubborn little terrier, and if anything I was the one sneaking him extra food when I wasn’t supposed to. My mother used to say he listened better than I did, which was probably true.\n\nBut no, I don’t remember anything like being physically cruel or breaking things on purpose or stealing. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget appointments, misplace important items like keys or your phone, or forget to complete tasks you had planned to do, in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… outside of work, it’s harder to say because there isn’t the same structure. With work, there’s a file, a client, a reason I have to keep things in one place—or at least pretend they’re in one place.\n\nAt home, if I put something down, sometimes I’ll find it in a completely different spot than I expected, but then again I live alone, so there’s no one else to blame. Well, except my cat, and she does knock things off tables, but she’s not exactly hiding my phone with intent.\n\nAppointments… I try to write them down. Whether I always remember where I wrote them is another matter. Sorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. I probably put it off sometimes, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do people often tell you that you seem to not be listening or that you drift off mentally when they’re speaking directly to you, even in moments when you’re not physically restless or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. People sometimes say my mind is somewhere else, but that could mean a lot of things, couldn’t it? In my line of work, half the time I’m listening to someone and also watching the door, or noticing what they didn’t say, or wondering why their story changed from Tuesday to Thursday.\n\nBut if you mean like… someone is talking right at me and I’m just gone? I don’t know that people tell me that often. My sister has said I get this look like I’m “three blocks away,” but she also says that when she’s telling me about neighborhood politics, and honestly there’s only so much one person can retain about parking disputes.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these patterns—being unable to sit still, interrupting others, or jumping in before your turn—cause noticeable problems for you in at least two different areas of your life, like both at work and in your personal relationships or home life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nAt work it’s starting to cost me. I’ve missed details in investigations because I jumped ahead or pushed too fast, and I’ve irritated clients—sometimes I can tell I’ve cut them off before they got to the part they thought mattered. And the junior detectives… some of them don’t want to debrief with me as much, I think, because they feel steamrolled. One of them actually used that word, which stuck with me.\n\nPersonal life… I mean, people close to me have their own opinions about how I come across, sure, but professionally is where it’s been hardest to brush off. I used to think it was just being sharp or efficient, but lately it’s more like I’m leaving dents."
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Notes in different places, reminders, little scraps—actually I found a receipt yesterday with half a license plate written on it, and I spent ten minutes trying to remember if it was important or just from some guy who cut me off in traffic.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it careless mistakes exactly.   }

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure. I mean, my energy goes up and down depending on what’s happening—if I’m chasing something on a case or waiting on somebody to call me back, I can get wired, but that feels more like the situation than some separate… episode.\n\nLess sleep, sure, sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say I suddenly don’t need it. It’s more like I stay up too late and then regret it, which is different, I think.\n\nRacing thoughts… my thoughts are usually pretty busy anyway. Sorry, I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t know if I’ve had a distinct several-day stretch that was really different from me. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
